EXPLANATION OF DISTRIBUTION OF FUNDS 

The conference agreement on the State and Local Fiscal Assistance Act of 1972, H.R. 14370, effective January 1, 1972, provides for the distribution of funds to State and local governments over a 5-year period. For the calendar year 1972, $5.3 billion is to be distributed to State and local governments. One-third of the $5.3 billion, approximately $1.8 billion, is to be distributed to State governments. 

The remaining two-thirds, approximately $3.5 billion, is to be distributed to the localities. 

The conference agreement provides that the annual amount distributed after the first fiscal year is to increase by $150 million a year.¹ 

Under the conference agreement, this increment is divided between the States and localities, one-third going to the States and two-thirds to the localities. The aggregate distribution under the conference agreement in the last half year of the 5-year period is to be at an annual rate of $6.65 billion.

For calendar year 1972, table 1 shows the estimated distributions under the conference agreement to the States for their own use and the total distribution to each of the States for their localities. Since the conference agreement provides that each State is to receive the higher amount received under the House or Senate agreement, table 1 also shows the House and Senate allocations by States and the result of taking the higher of these two. As is indicated in table 1, this results in a total which is in excess of $5.3 billion. The conference agreement provides for the scaling of the amount going to each State down on a pro rata basis so that the total remains at $5.3 billion. This is the total amount shown in column 4 going to States and localities for the calendar year 1972.

The distributions to individual localities within each State under the conference agreement are shown in the tabular material which comprises the bulk of this report (beginning at the end of this introductory explanation). The distributions were prepared by the staff of the Treasury Department.

The State data in this report are arranged alphabetically with the breakdown of the amount going to the localities within each State classified by county area (again arranged alphabetically). Municipalities are classified under the county in which they are located. 

The data shown are estimates for calendar year 1972, the first two 

6-month entitlement periods. 

1The increment in the last half year of the 5-year period is to be at an annual rate of $300 million instead of $150 million. (V)

Local share includes amounts redistributed to the States because of limitation. These amounts are shown in the tabulation which follows on the 1st page for each State. 

Note: Details may not add to totals because of rounding. 

In general, the $5.3 billion initially distributed to the States under the conference agreement is distributed under the House formula or under the Senate formula, whichever results in the higher amount for a State. Each States distribution is then reduced on a proportionate basis so that the total amount distributed in the first year of distribution is $5.3 billion.

VII Under the House formula $1.8 billion is distributed to the State governments on an incentive basis, one-half designed to encourage tax effort generally and one-half to encourage greater use of individual income taxes. General tax effort for the first portion is measured by comparing State and local tax collections to the personal income level in one State, with similar revenue efforts made by the other States. 

Income tax encouragement in the second portion is provided by distributing $900 million based on 15 percent of a State's individual income tax collections. However, in no event is the minimum amount taken into account to be less than 1 percent of total Federal income tax liabilities within the State. Nor may the maximum amount exceed 

6 percent of these Federal income tax liabilities. Under the House formula, $3.5 billion (approximately two-thirds of $5.3 billion) is distributed among the States for use of local governments. The distribution among the States is based one-third on population, one-third on urbanized population and one-third on population weighted by inverse relative income levels. These same factors also determine the distribution to local governments within a State under the House formula.

Under the Senate distribution, the formula for distribution among the States is population times tax effort times inverse relative income levels.2 

One-third of the total amount distributed to a State under the conference agreement remains with the State governments for their own use. The remaining two-thirds is distributed to county areas under the formula population times tax effort times inverse relative income levels. The county area funds are then divided between the county government and the aggregate of the municipalities in the county area on the basis of the adjusted revenues raised by each.³ The division of the funds among the municipalities is made under the same formula as the division among the county areas. 

The conference agreement provides that in no event is the per capita amount going to any country area, municipal government, or township government to be less than 20 percent of the average per capita local amount for the State as a whole or more than 145 percent of this average. In addition, the bill provides that in no event is the amount of revenue sharing funds going to any county government or municipality to be more than 50 percent of adjusted taxes from its own sources plus intergovernmental transfers. The same type of division as that made for municipalities is to be made for townships where these types of governmental units exist and raise taxes. The term "adjusted taxes" referred to here is taxes reduced by those set aside, or used, for education. 

After the first year, the formulas indicated above used within a State may be modified by the State (by statute) where this is considered desirable to meet local problems. The States may substitute for the general formula in the conference agreement a formula consisting of two factors: population multiplied by tax effort and population multiplied by inverse relative income levels. These factors may be weighed 

2 An additional amount equal to 25 percent or 15 percent (depending upon the application of the law in their cases) of the amount otherwise computed goes to noncontiguous States (namely, Alaska and Hawaii), where cost-of-living grants are in effect. 

3A special distribution in some cases is made to Indian tribes before this allocation. 

This distribution is made on the basis of population.

VIII in any manner deemed desirable by the State. If the State substitutes an alternative formula for that provided in the conference agreement, it may not, however, in the 5-year period subsequently change this formula.

Although distributions will be made to municipalities with a population under 2,500, the breakdown for the smaller municipalities is not yet available. Similarly, the distribution for townships also is not yet generally available. Distributions will, however, be made to any municipalities or townships where its entitlement amounts to $200 or more.

Under the conference agreement, the Treasury is authorized, however, to make distributions to communities with populations of 500 or less on the basis of population only. 

Distributions will be made to municipalities for any entitlement period if the municipality is incorporated before the beginning of that period. In other words, for the entitlement period beginning 

January 1, 1972, and ending June 30, 1972, a municipality will be included in the distribution if it was organized on or before December 31, 1971. In the case of municipalities which are newly incorporated, it probably will not be possible to make the distribution to them on time. However, as subsequent information becomes available, the distribution will be made to the newly incorporated municipality if its existence began before the entitlement period in question. This will also require subsequent adjustment in the amounts going to a county government or other municipalities where one of these late payments is made to a newly incorporated municipality. 

As has been indicated where part or all of an Indian tribe is located within a county, distributions may be made to the tribe (on a population basis) before distributions are made to any other regular governmental units within the county area. Because it appears that there may be some delay in obtaining adequate information with respect to Indian tribal population in at least some counties, this may result in a delay in the distribution to the tribal governments, and where they are a significant part of the county might also mean a postponement of payments to other governmental units within the county area. The distributions to Indian tribes are not reflected in the distributions presented in this report.

The tabulation which follows shows, by States, the grants from the Federal Government to all local governments within each State. This tabulation is arranged alphabetically by State, and the table of contents, at the beginning of this explanation, indicates the pages on which the data for each State begin. 

The information shown, and order in which shown, with respect to each State is as follows: (1) The total grants to local governments within the State. (2) The amounts returned to the State government to use as it sees fit because of the application of the 50 percent limitation to local governments. These amounts may ultimately be somewhat larger than shown since there may be amounts which municipalities under 2,500 in population and townships may not receive because of the limitation.

IX (3) The total amount going to all governmental units within each county area. The county areas are arranged alphabetically within each State. (4) The total amount going to the county government itself. (5) The total amount going to all municipal governments in the county with populations of over 2,500. (6) The total amount going to all municipal governments with populations of under 2,500. (7) The total amount going to all townships in the county. 

Where there are no townships or the township government does not raise any revenue, a "O" is shown for this entry. (8) A breakdown of the amount going to each municipal government within the county with a population of over 2,500 is shown. (Distributions also are to be made to smaller municipalities, but this is not shown in this tabulation.) Some municipalities are located in more than one county. Where this is true, the name of the city is followed by the word “(Part).” In such a case, it is necessary, in order to obtain the total payment going to the city, to add together the amounts going to it from the two or more applicable county areas.
